Zero-viscosity limit of the chemotaxis-Navier-Stokes equations with the Navier-slip boundary condition
Bolun Li, Fengqiang Shi, Wendong Wang

Abstract
. The interplay of chemotaxis and diffusion of nutrients or signaling chemicals in bacterial suspensions can produce a variety of structures with locally high concentrations of cells, including phyllotactic patterns, filaments, and concentrations in fabricated microstructures, which is described by the chemotaxis-Navier-Stokes flow by Tuval et al. in 2005. Dombrowski et al. also observed that Bacterial flow in a sessile drop related to those in the Boycott effect of sedimentation can carry bioconvective plumes, viewed from below through the bottom of a petri dish, and the horizontal “turbulence” white line near the top is the air-water-plastic contact line. It’s interesting to verify these turbulent phenomena mathematically. For varying chemotactic and velocity viscosities, we derive the boundary layer equations of the chemotaxis-Navier-Stokes system rigorously in a two-dimensional half-space under the Navier-slip boundary condition and obtain the vanishing viscosity limit of the 2D chemotaxis-fluid coupled system in the anisotropic conormal Sobolev spaces.



Keywords:
Chemotaxics Navier-Stokes system, Boundary layer, Vanishing viscosity limit, Asymptotic expansion, Conormal spaces

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.

Primary 35Q92; Secondary 76N05, 35B44, 35Q30, 35Q35

1.
Introduction

The chemotactic movement of microorganisms in a fluid represents a fundamental coupling between biology and fluid dynamics, where the directed motion of organisms (chemotaxis) interacts with the surrounding flow. To capture this interaction, Keller and Segel
[
13
,
14
]
established the classical Keller-Segel model in the 1970s (see, also, Patlak
[
30
]
). The physical significance of this coupling was dramatically demonstrated by Dombrowski et al.
[
3
]
and Tuval et al.
[
39
]
, who observed that aerobic bacteria aggregate near the water surface, forming a distinct boundary layer and triggering large-scale bioconvection and micro-turbulence. These experiments reveal that the presence of a fluid fundamentally alters bacterial spatial organization, making the study of boundary layers essential for understanding such systems. Although extensive mathematical results exist for chemotaxis-fluid equations
[
44
,
45
]
, the boundary layer analysis or the vanishing viscosity limit remains largely unexplored. Investigating this limit is not only mathematically challenging but also physically significant, as it reveals the mechanism by which a nearly inviscid fluid organizes microbial life at its boundary.

To address the vanishing viscosity limit in chemotaxis-fluid systems, we first draw insights from classical Navier-Stokes equations. Since Prandtl
[
34
]

introduced the boundary layer theory and derived the Prandtl equation by the
asymptotic expansion in 1904,
a fundamental problem in mathematical fluid dynamics is to determine whether
in the inviscid limit
\nu\rightarrow 0
the solutions of the Navier–Stokes equations converge
to those of the Euler equations. Let us recall some developments briefly.

To justify the Prandtl boundary layer expansion, one of the key steps is to
establish the well-posedness of the Prandtl equation.
Under the monotonicity assumption on the outflow velocity, Oleinik and Samokhin
[
29
]
proved the local existence and uniqueness of classical solutions in the two-dimensional case. For flows with a favorable pressure gradient, Xin and Zhang
[
46
]
established the global existence of weak solutions. More recently, the local well-posedness in Sobolev spaces was independently proved by Alexandre et al.
[
1
]
and Masmoudi and Wong
[
27
]
using direct energy methods.
On the other hand, within the analytic setting, Sammartino and Caflisch
[
35
]
obtained the local existence and uniqueness of solutions for full analytic data (with extensions to tangential analytic data found in
[
23
,
47
]
). For the Gevrey data, the local well-posed results were obtained by Gerard-Varet-Masmoudi
[
6
]
and Li Yang
[
21
]
. Conversely, without the monotonicity condition, the system exhibits strong instability; notably, Gérard-Varet and Dormy
[
5
]
demonstrated that the linearized Prandtl equation around non-monotonic shear flows is ill-posed in Sobolev spaces. For more developments on this topic, we refer to
[
47
,
22
,
20
]
and the references therein.

Another key step is the rigorous verification of the Prandtl boundary layer
expansion.
Even when the Prandtl equation is well-posed in the aforementioned functional spaces, rigorously verifying the Prandtl boundary layer expansion remains highly challenging, and only a few landmark results are available. Sammartino and Caflisch
[
35
]
first achieved this justification within the analytic framework, a result later re-proved by Wang, Wang, and Zhang
[
41
]
via a newly developed direct energy method. Moreover, the result was extended to the three-dimensional half-space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
by Fei, Tao, and Zhang
[
4
]
utilizing a direct energy approach again. Moving beyond analytic spaces, Maekawa
[
25
]
successfully justified the boundary layer expansion under the specific condition that the initial vorticity is supported away from the boundary. We refer to
[
17
,
16
]
and the references therein for more recent progress.

1.1.
Chemotaxis equations

Parallel to the development of boundary layer theory in fluid dynamics, few significant progress has also been made in the study of pure chemotaxis equations. The classical Keller-Segel system
[
13
,
14
]
has been extensively analyzed, particularly regarding the critical balance between chemotactic aggregation and diffusion that leads to either global existence or finite-time blow-up
[
7
]
. Hillen and Painter
[
7
]
provided a comprehensive review of global existence and blow-up phenomena in chemotaxis PDE models.

1.1.1.
The case of chemotaxis equations without fluid coupling

By assuming the Neumann boundary condition, Meng-Xu-Wang
[
28
]
considered the boundary layer problem of a Keller-Segel model in a domain of two dimensional space with vanishing chemical diffusion coefficient:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{\varepsilon}-\Delta n^{\varepsilon}=-\nabla\cdot(n^{\varepsilon}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}),\\
&\partial_{t}c^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta c^{\varepsilon}=-c^{\varepsilon}n^{\varepsilon},\\
&\partial_{y}c^{\varepsilon}=\partial_{y}n^{\varepsilon}=0,\quad{\rm on}~y=0\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

and they obtained the error equations
(n,c)=(n^{\varepsilon}-n^{a},c^{\varepsilon}-c^{a})
satisfying

\displaystyle\|n\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}-\delta},\quad\|c\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta},

(1.2)

where
\delta>0.

Moreover, Hou-Wang
[
8
]
established the convergence of boundary
layer solutions of the Keller-Segel model with singular sensitivity:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n=\nabla\cdot(D\nabla n-\chi\frac{n}{c}\nabla c)\\
&\partial_{t}c=\varepsilon\Delta c-nc,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.3)

and by assuming the Dirichlet-Neumann boundary condiotion they justify that the boundary layer converges to
the outer layer (solution with
\varepsilon
= 0) plus the inner layer with the convergence rate of
(n,c)
as
(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}},\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}})
.
Lee-Wang-Yang
[
18
]
also considered the stationary
problem of Keller-Segel model, which is reduced to a singularly perturbed nonlocal semi-linear elliptic
problem.
Carrillo-Hong-Wang
[
2
]

show that the model with physical boundary conditions proposed
by Tuval et al. in one dimension can generate a boundary layer solution as the oxygen diffusion rate
\varepsilon
is small. Specifically, we show that the solution of the model with

\varepsilon>0
will converge to the solution with
\varepsilon=0
(outer-layer solution) plus the boundary
layer profiles (inner-layer solution) with a sharp transition near the boundary as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
For more developments on general domains, we refer to
[
33
,
31
,
32
,
19
]
and the references therein.

1.1.2.
The case of chemotaxis equations with fluid coupling

Only a few studies have explored boundary layer problems in chemotaxis-fluid systems. In 2024, Hou
[
11
]
considered the vanishing diffusion limit issue for the chemotaxis–Navier–Stokes system in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with the vanishing chemical diffusion rate.
Hou
[
10
]
further demonstrated that the boundary layer effect in the chemotaxis-Navier-Stokes system as follows:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &n_{t}+\vec{u}\cdot\nabla n+\nabla\cdot(n\nabla c)=\Delta n,&&(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\times(0,T),\\
&c_{t}+\vec{u}\cdot\nabla c+nc=\varepsilon\Delta c,&&(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\times(0,T),\\
&\vec{u}_{t}+\vec{u}\cdot\nabla\vec{u}+\nabla p+(0,\lambda)n=\Delta\vec{u},&&(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\times(0,T),\\
&\nabla\cdot\vec{u}=0,&&(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\times(0,T),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.4)

with the boundary conditions

\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\nabla n-n\nabla c)\cdot\vec{n}=0,\quad\nabla c\cdot\vec{n}=0,\quad\vec{u}=\mathbf{0}&&\text{if }\varepsilon>0,\\
&(\nabla n-n\nabla c)\cdot\vec{n}=0,\quad\vec{u}=\mathbf{0}&&\text{if }\varepsilon=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.5)

where the author obtained the convergence rate is

\displaystyle\left\|\left(n^{\varepsilon}-n^{0},c^{\varepsilon}-c^{0}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty}_{xy})}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\left\|\left(\vec{u}^{\varepsilon}-\vec{u}^{0}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty}_{xy})}\leq C\varepsilon,

(1.6)

which
(n^{0},c^{0},\vec{u}^{0})
is the solution of (
1.4
) with
\varepsilon=0
.
Despite these advances, the vanishing viscosity limit for chemotaxis-fluid systems remains an open challenge. Biologically, chemotaxis is inseparable from the surrounding fluid dynamics; thus, the formation of the fluid boundary layer inevitably dictates the behavior of the chemotactic boundary layer. As both fluid and chemotactic viscosities vanish, the strong coupling of these boundary layers creates a highly singular region. A fundamental question naturally emerges:

is it mathematically possible to establish a rigorous boundary layer expansion in this regime, thereby offering a mathematical answer to the complex phenomena reported in Dombrowski et. al.’s experiments in
[
3
]
?

1.2.
Boundary layers in the chemotaxis system

Motivated by these physical and mathematical challenges, this paper aims to systematically investigate the vanishing viscosity-diffusivity limits and the associated boundary layer expansions for the chemotaxis-fluid equations on the two-dimensional half-plane
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
.
Consider the model coupling the chemotaxis equations for bacterial density
n^{\varepsilon}
and oxygen concentration
c^{\varepsilon}
with the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations for the velocity field
u^{\varepsilon}
, as shown below:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla n^{\varepsilon}-\Delta n^{\varepsilon}=-\nabla\cdot(n^{\varepsilon}\nabla c^{\varepsilon}),\\
&\partial_{t}c^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla c^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta c^{\varepsilon}=-c^{\varepsilon}n^{\varepsilon},\\
&\partial_{t}u^{\varepsilon}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta u^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}=n^{\varepsilon}\nabla\phi,\\
&\nabla\cdot u^{\varepsilon}=0,\\
&\partial_{y}c^{\varepsilon}=\partial_{y}n^{\varepsilon}=0,~\partial_{y}u^{\varepsilon}_{1}=u^{\varepsilon}_{2}=0,\quad y=0,\\
&(n^{\varepsilon},c^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})=(n_{in},c_{in},u_{in}),\quad t=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.7)

Here, we assume that
\phi=y
without loss of generality, then
\nabla\phi=(0,1)^{\top}=e_{2}
, and the system is supplemented with physically reasonable Neumann-slip boundary conditions
[
43
]
and nonnegative initial data
n_{in},c_{in}\geq 0
.

Formally, as the viscosity coefficient
\varepsilon\to 0
, the solution of (
1.7
) converges to the following inviscid limit system on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{0}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla n^{0}-\Delta n^{0}=-\nabla\cdot(n^{0}\nabla c^{0}),\\
&\partial_{t}c^{0}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla c^{0}=-c^{0}n^{0},\\
&\partial_{t}u^{0}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla u^{0}+\nabla p^{0}=n^{0}e_{2},\\
&\nabla\cdot u^{0}=0,\\
&\partial_{y}n^{0}=n^{0}\partial_{y}c^{0},\quad u_{2}^{0}=0,\quad y=0,\\
&(u^{0},c^{0},n^{0})=(n_{in},c_{in},u_{in}),\quad t=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.8)

However, due to the mismatch of normal velocity boundary conditions between the viscous and inviscid systems, boundary layers may appear.

This research will follow the paradigm of matched asymptotic expansion method. First, we introduce boundary layer coordinates
z=y/\varepsilon
and decompose the solution into an outer solution describing the flow far from the boundary and an internal correction term describing boundary layer behavior. Through asymptotic expansion of the original equations, we derive the equations satisfied by each order of outer solutions and internal correction terms, and construct approximate solutions
(n^{a},u^{a},c^{a},p^{a})
. The core of subsequent work will be to prove that the error between this approximate solution and the true solution of the original equation tends to zero as
\varepsilon\to 0
. To this end, we plan to introduce an anisotropic Sobolev space that can assign different weights to tangential (
x
) and normal (
y
) derivatives, thereby finely capturing the characteristics of drastic normal variations within the boundary layer
[
9
,
43
]
. By establishing energy inequalities for error variables and using Gronwall-type lemmas, we hope to obtain energy estimates, thereby strictly quantifying convergence rates. This energy estimation method draws on the techniques of Masmoudi and Rousset
[
26
]
in handling Navier slip boundary conditions, as well as the energy construction ideas of Wang, Wang and Zhang
[
41
]
in the analytic framework, and innovatively applies them to complex systems coupled with nonlinear chemotaxis terms. Ultimately, this research will for the first time rigorously verify from a mathematical perspective the boundary layer asymptotic structure of a class of chemotaxis-fluid equations under the vanishing viscosity limit, revealing how fluid viscosity and biological chemotaxis jointly act to cause the fine distribution of bacterial density and oxygen concentration near the boundary.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce the functional framework, formulate the error equations, and state our main results. Section
3
is devoted to stating the key
a priori
estimates and providing the proof of the main theorem. Subsequently, in Section
4
, we detail the derivation of the asymptotic expansions and rigorously construct the approximate solutions. In Section
5
, we establish the necessary supporting lemmas and prove the
a priori
estimates presented in Section
3
. Finally, the well-posedness proofs for the inner and outer layer solutions are deferred to the Appendix.

In this paper, to simplify notation, we let
h(\cdot)
represent an undetermined function. Then the boundary operator is expressed as
\overline{h}(t,x)=h(t,x,0)
, and the shift operator is expressed as:

\binom{h_{1}(\cdot,j)}{h_{2}(\cdot,j)}_{p}=\binom{h_{1}(\cdot,j)}{h_{2}(\cdot,j+1)},~\binom{h_{1}(\cdot,j)}{h_{2}(\cdot,j)}_{pp}=\binom{h_{1}(\cdot,j)}{h_{2}(\cdot,j+2)},

(1.9)

and when
j<0
,
h(\cdot,j)=0
. Throughout this paper, we denote by
C
a constant independent of
\varepsilon
and by
C_{\xi}
a coefficient dependent only on
\xi
, which may be different from line to line.

2.
Error equations, functional spaces and main results

2.1.
The error equations

Assume the approximate solution
(n^{a},c^{a},u^{a},p^{a})
satisfies:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{a}+u^{a}\cdot\nabla n^{a}-\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla n^{a}-n^{a}\nabla c^{a}\right)=-N,\\
&\partial_{t}c^{a}+u^{a}\cdot\nabla c^{a}+c^{a}n^{a}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta c^{a}=-K,\\
&\partial_{t}u^{a}+u^{a}\cdot\nabla u^{a}+\nabla p^{a}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta u^{a}-n^{a}e_{2}=-U,\\
&\nabla\cdot u^{a}=0,\\
&\partial_{y}n^{a}=\partial_{y}c^{a}=\partial_{y}u_{1}^{a}=0,u_{2}^{a}=\varepsilon^{2}f(t,x),\quad y=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(2.10)

where
(N,C,U)
are remainders which are small in some functional space.

We introduce the error between the solution and the approximate solution:

n=n^{\varepsilon}-n^{a},c=c^{\varepsilon}-c^{a},u=u^{\varepsilon}-u^{a},p=p^{\varepsilon}-p^{a}.

Then from (
1.7
) and (
2.10
), we know that
(n,c,u,p)
satisfies:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n+\left(u^{a}\cdot\nabla n+u\cdot\nabla n^{a}+u\cdot\nabla n\right)+\nabla\cdot\left(n\nabla c+n^{a}\nabla c+n\nabla c^{a}\right)\\
&\quad-\Delta n=N,\\
&\partial_{t}c+\left(u^{a}\cdot\nabla c+u\cdot\nabla c^{a}+u\cdot\nabla c\right)-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta c+\left(c^{a}n+cn^{a}+cn\right)=K,\\
&\partial_{t}u+\left(u^{a}\cdot\nabla u+u\cdot\nabla u^{a}+u\cdot\nabla u\right)+\nabla p-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta u-ne_{2}=U,\\
&\nabla\cdot u=0,\\
&\partial_{y}n=\partial_{y}c=\partial_{y}u_{1}=0,~u_{2}=-\varepsilon^{2}f,\quad y=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.11)

Taking the divergence operator on both sides of (
2.11
)
3
, we obtain the pressure equation:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\Delta p=\nabla\cdot\left((u^{a}+u)\cdot\nabla u\right)+\nabla\cdot\left(u\cdot\nabla u^{a}\right)-\partial_{y}n-\nabla\cdot U,\\
&\partial_{y}p=U_{2}-\partial_{t}u_{2}-(u^{a}+u)\cdot\nabla u_{2}-u\cdot\nabla u_{2}^{a}+\varepsilon^{2}\Delta u_{2}+n,,\quad y=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.12)

The vorticity
\omega,\omega^{a}
is defined by:

\omega=\partial_{y}u_{1}-\partial_{x}u_{2},~\omega^{a}=\partial_{y}u_{1}^{a}-\partial_{x}u_{2}^{a}.

(2.13)

Then from (
2.11
)
3
we can deduce that:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\omega+\left(u^{a}\cdot\nabla\omega+u\cdot\nabla\omega^{a}+u\cdot\nabla\omega\right)-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta\omega+\partial_{x}n=-\nabla^{\perp}\cdot U,\\
&\omega=\partial_{y}u_{1}-\partial_{x}u_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}f,\quad y=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.14)

2.2.
Differential operator and functional spaces

As in
[
26
,
27
,
37
]
, we introduce the conormal derivative:

\partial^{\alpha}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{t}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\psi(y)^{\alpha_{3}}\partial_{y}^{\alpha_{3}},

(2.15)

where
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{3})
is a multi-index and
\psi(y)
is a smooth function defined by:

\displaystyle\psi(y)=\begin{cases}\delta y,&\text{for }y\leq\frac{1}{2},\\
\frac{\delta y}{1+y},&\text{for }y\geq 1,\end{cases}

(2.16)

where
\delta>0
, to be decided later.

Here we say that
\beta\leq\alpha
in
\mathbb{N}^{3}
if
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{3}),\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3})
satisfy
\beta_{1}\leq\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}\leq\alpha_{2},\beta_{3}\leq\alpha_{3}
.
In addition, there is also the gradient operator:

\nabla=\binom{\partial_{x}}{\partial_{y}},\quad\widehat{\nabla}=\binom{\partial_{x}}{\partial_{z}}.

where
z=\frac{y}{\varepsilon}
.
Define the commutator of two operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
as
[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}-\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}
.
Then we have

[\partial_{y},\partial^{\alpha}]=\alpha_{3}\psi^{\prime}\partial^{\alpha-(0,0,1)}\partial_{y}.

(2.17)

(If
\alpha_{3}=0
, we denote
[\partial_{y},\partial^{\alpha}]=0
.)

Denote
\left\|g\right\|_{q}=\|g\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}
. For
l,m\in\mathbb{N}
, the conormal Sobolev spaces are defined as follows:

\displaystyle Y^{l,m}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\right)=\left\{u\;\Bigg|\;\|u\|_{Y^{l,m}}^{2}=\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq l,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}u\|_{2}^{2}<\infty\right\},

(2.18)

\displaystyle Y^{l,m}_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\right)=\left\{u\;\Bigg|\;\|u\|_{Y_{\infty}^{l,m}}^{2}=\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq l,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}u\|_{\infty}^{2}<\infty\right\},

\displaystyle Y^{l}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\right)=\left\{u\;\Bigg|\;\|u\|_{Y^{l}}^{2}=\sum_{\tau\leq l}\|\partial^{\tau}_{t}u\|_{2}^{2}<\infty\right\}.

Similarly, denote the general Sobolev spaces by

\displaystyle\overline{H}^{l,m}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\right)=\left\{u\;\Bigg|\;\|u\|_{\overline{H}^{l,m}}^{2}=\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq l,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{t}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{y}^{\alpha_{3}}u\|_{2}^{2}<\infty\right\},

(2.19)

\displaystyle H^{m}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\right)=\left\{u\;\Bigg|\;\|u\|_{H^{m}}^{2}=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{y}^{\alpha_{3}}u\|_{2}^{2}<\infty\right\}.

2.3.
Main results

Let us first introduce a proposition on approximate solutions and remainders:

Proposition 2.1
.

Assuming
(n_{in},c_{in},u_{in})\in H^{9}
satisfy the compatibility conditions
(\mathcal{I}^{3})
(see Section
6.1
for details),
and
(n^{a},c^{a},u^{a})
takes the form

\left\{\begin{aligned} n^{a}(t,x,y)&=n^{e,0}(t,x,y)+\varepsilon n^{b,1}(t,x,z)+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}(t,x,z),\\
c^{a}(t,x,y)&=c^{e,0}(t,x,y)+\varepsilon c^{b,1}(t,x,z)+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}(t,x,z),\\
u_{1}^{a}(t,x,y)&=u_{1}^{e,0}(t,x,y)+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}(t,x,z),\\
u_{2}^{a}(t,x,y)&=u_{2}^{e,0}(t,x,y)+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}(t,x,z),\\
p^{a}(t,x,y)&=p^{e,0}(t,x,y)+\varepsilon^{2}p^{b,2}(t,x,z),\end{aligned}\right.

(2.20)

then it follows that:

•

Uniform bounds of approximate solutions:

There exist
T_{a}>0
such that for any
t\in[0,T_{a}]

\displaystyle\|n^{a}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}Y^{1,3}_{\infty}}+\|c^{a}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}Y^{1,4}_{\infty}}+\|u^{a}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}Y^{1,4}_{\infty}}\leq C,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}n^{a}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}Y^{1,2}_{\infty}}+\|\partial_{y}c^{a}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}Y^{1,3}_{\infty}}+\|\partial_{y}u^{a}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}Y^{1,3}_{\infty}}\leq C,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}^{2}c^{a}\|_{L_{t}^{2}Y^{1,2}}+\|\partial_{y}^{2}u^{a}\|_{L_{t}^{2}Y^{1,2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

•

Uniform bounds of the remainders:

For all
t\in[0,T_{a}]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|N\|_{L_{t}^{2}Y^{1,2}}+\|K\|_{L_{t}^{2}Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla K\|_{L_{t}^{2}Y^{1,2}}+\|U\|_{L_{t}^{2}Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{\perp}\cdot U\|_{L_{t}^{2}Y^{1,2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}.

(2.22)

•

Uniform bounds of
f
:

There exists
F(t,x)
such that for
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
f(t,x)=\partial_{x}F
with

\displaystyle\|fe^{-y}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}Y^{2,{5}}}+\|Fe^{-y}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}Y^{2,2}}\leq C.

(2.23)

Proof.

The proof can be found in the Appendix (see Subsection
6.3
).

MM

\square

Our main result starts as follows.

Theorem 2.2
.

Assume Proposition
2.1
holds. Let
\omega
be defined as in (
2.13
). Then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
T>0
, and
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,
t\in[0,T]
, the error equation (
2.11
) admits a unique solution
(n,c,u)(t,\cdot)\in Y^{1,2}
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\|n\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\nabla n\|_{L^{2}_{t}Y^{1,2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}},

\displaystyle\|c\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\nabla c\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\nabla^{2}c\|_{L^{2}_{t}Y^{1,2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}},

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\nabla\omega\|_{L^{2}_{t}Y^{1,2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}.

In particular, we have

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|(n,c,u)(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}.

In Section 3,
we construct the approximate solution
(n^{a},c^{a},u^{a},p^{a})
of (
1.7
) by using the matched asymptotic expansion, which satisfies Proposition
2.1
. Thus, we have the following conclusion.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let Proposition
2.1
holds, then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
T>0
, and
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, the chemotaxis-Navier-Stokes system (
1.7
) admit a unique solution
(n^{\varepsilon},c^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})
on
[0,T]
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\|n^{\varepsilon}-n^{a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\nabla(n^{\varepsilon}-n^{a})\|_{L^{2}_{t}Y^{1,2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}},

\displaystyle\|c^{\varepsilon}-c^{a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\nabla(c^{\varepsilon}-c^{a})\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\nabla^{2}(c^{\varepsilon}-c^{a})\|_{L^{2}_{t}Y^{1,2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}},

\displaystyle\|u^{\varepsilon}-u^{a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\omega^{\varepsilon}-\omega^{a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}+\|\nabla(\omega^{\varepsilon}-\omega^{a})\|_{L^{2}_{t}Y^{1,2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}.

In particular, we have

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|(n^{\varepsilon},c^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})(t,\cdot)-(n^{a},c^{a},u^{a})(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Remark 2.4

(Difficulties in the construction of boundary layer corrections )
.

The above theorem implies the
L^{\infty}
strong convergence from the system (
1.7
) to a constructed solution, which seems to be the first result of vanishing limit for the chemotaxis-Navier-Stokes system with chemotactic viscosity and fluid viscosity.
One main difficulty lies in the construction of boundary layer corrections
n^{b,j}
: a direct attempt to solve the equation for
\partial_{t}n^{b,j}
is difficult, as it contains the higher-order derivative
\partial_{z}^{2}n^{b,j+2}
. This creates a circular dependency where the equation for a low-order correction explicitly involves an unknown higher-order term.
To overcome this obstacle, we instead exploit the equation of the lower-order term
\partial_{t}n^{b,j-2}
. A key observation is that this equation induces a degenerate equation for
n^{b,j}
: the time derivative of
n^{b,j}
no longer appears, and the equation reduces to a linear relation involving only
\partial_{z}^{2}n^{b,j}
and known lower-order quantities.
In details,
in order to estimate
n^{b,1}
, we identify the useful structure:

\displaystyle\partial_{z}n^{b,1}-\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left(n^{E,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}+n^{b,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{E,k}+n^{b,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}\right)=0,

(see (
4.48
)). Notably, all terms in this equation are of order at most
1
. As a result,
n^{b,1}
can be solved by combining the equation for
c^{b,1}
with other known lower-order terms. The only additional requirement is the well-posedness of the system of
(n^{e,0},c^{e,0},u^{e,0})
, whose boundary values can also be derived from this structure (see (
4.50
)).
Similarly, for
n^{b,2}
we use the following relation:

\displaystyle\partial_{z}n^{b,2}=\left(n^{E,1}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}+n^{b,1}\partial_{z}c^{E,1}+n^{b,1}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}\right)+n^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,2}

(see (
4.57
)). Hence, the solving of
n^{b,2}
can be obtained by using the equations of
c^{b,2}
and
(n^{e,1},c^{e,1},u^{e,1})
, whose boundary condition is derived as in (
4.58
).
For the detailed constructing of the approximate solution and the derivation process, we refer to Figure
1
as follows.

(u_{2}^{b,0},p^{b,0})
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\partial_{y}n^{e,0}-n^{e,0}\partial_{y}c^{e,0}\big|_{y=0}=0
(c^{b,0},n^{b,0},u_{1}^{b,0})
(n^{e,0},c^{e,0},u^{e,0})
(u_{2}^{b,1},p^{b,1})
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\partial_{y}n^{e,1}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{1}n^{e,1-k}\partial_{y}c^{e,k}\big|_{y=0}=0
(n^{b,1},c^{b,1},u_{1}^{b,1})
(n^{e,1},c^{e,1},u^{e,1})
(u_{2}^{b,2},p^{b,2})
(n^{b,2},c^{b,2},u_{1}^{b,2})

Figure 1.

Remark 2.5

(The sharp convergence rate)
.

A key observation is that although the approximate solution is expanded up to second-order boundary layer corrections, it yields a suboptimal convergence rate of
O(\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}})
, which is better that the rate in (
1.6
) obtained in
[
10
]
. This limitation is primarily driven by two analytical factors:

•

First, due to the
degeneracy
of the equations governing
n^{b,j}
discussed in Remark
2.4
, truncating the expansion at
n^{b,2}
inevitably relegates the term
\varepsilon\partial_{t}n^{b,1}
to the remainder term
N
. As demonstrated in Proposition
6.10
, this restricts the norm of
N
to
O(\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}})
.

•

Second, evaluating the normal derivatives
\partial_{y}K
and
\partial_{y}U_{1}
introduces an inherent loss of
\varepsilon
scaling. Specifically, the boundary layer scaling
\partial_{y}=\varepsilon^{-1}\partial_{z}
consumes one power of
\varepsilon
, which ultimately caps the overall convergence rate at
O(\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}})
.

Notably, if the approximate solution were only constructed up to the first-order correction
n^{b,1}
, the convergence rate would severely degrade to
O(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Remark 2.6

(Difficulties in a priori estimates)
.

We highlight three major technical difficulties encountered in our
a priori
estimates and the strategies used to overcome them:

•

Treatment of
n
in the error equations:
Since the anisotropic conormal space
Y^{0,m}
does not embed into
L_{x,y}^{\infty}
, controlling the
L_{x,y}^{\infty}
-norm of
n
requires at least one additional normal derivative,
\partial_{y}
. However, directly estimating
\|\partial_{y}n\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x,y}}
would necessitate bounding third-order normal derivatives of boundary layer profiles (e.g.,
\partial_{z}^{3}c^{b,1}
and
\partial_{z}^{3}u^{b,1}_{1}
), leading to severe boundary complications. To bypass this, we utilize Sobolev embedding in time, motivating the inclusion of time derivatives in our anisotropic conormal spaces
Y^{l,m}
. Under this framework, the estimates are closed by simply controlling
\|\partial_{y}n\|_{L_{t}^{2}Y^{1,m}}
.

•

Outer layer boundary conditions:
The leading-order outer layer equation, derived via asymptotic expansions, satisfies a zero-flux boundary condition (
4.51
). This is inherently weaker than the free-slip condition of the original viscous system. To address this, we rewrite the equation as

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{2}n^{e,0}=\partial_{t}n^{e,0}+u^{e,0}\cdot\nabla n^{e,0}-\partial_{x}^{2}n^{e,0}+\nabla\cdot(n^{e,0}\nabla c^{e,0}),

(see (
6.143
)), which allows us to recover the normal regularity of
n^{e,0}
via time and tangential derivatives.

•

Initial data regularity:
The higher regularity assumptions imposed on the initial data are specifically tailored to establish the convergence of the boundary layer expansions. These conditions could be relaxed if the goal were solely to prove the existence of solutions.

Remark 2.7

(Origins of the boundary layer)
.

Similar to the findings reported in
[
10
]
, the boundary layer effect in the present system is induced by the presence of the fluid rather than the chemotactic mechanism alone. To verify this, we consider the chemotaxis-only subsystem by formally setting
u=\nabla p=\mathbf{0}
in (
1.7
). From the chemical concentration equation with
\varepsilon=0
, we can deduce

\displaystyle\overline{\partial_{y}c^{0}}(t,x)=\overline{\partial_{y}c^{0}}(0,x)e^{-\int_{0}^{t}[\overline{n^{0}}(1+\overline{c^{0}})](\tau,x)d\tau}=0,

thus by (
4.66
), (
4.70
) we have
c^{b,1}=n^{b,1}=c^{b,2}=n^{b,2}\equiv 0
through the uniqueness of solutions.
In this case, the boundary layer correction terms for the chemotaxis variables vanish identically.

3.
Key energy estimates and proof of main result

3.1.
Key energy estimates

The key is to prove that the solution of error equation (
2.11
) is uniformly bounded in the suitable functional spaces. Now we present some main propositions.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
m\geq 2\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exist
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|n\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+(1-C\delta-C\sigma)\|\nabla n\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}-C\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla^{2}c\|_{Y^{1,1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{\sigma}\left(1+\|n^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|u^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla c^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}^{2}\right)^{2}\times

\displaystyle\quad\left(\|n\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)

(3.24)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C}{\sigma^{3}\varepsilon^{4}}\left(\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|n\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle\quad+C\varepsilon^{4}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}^{2}\left(\|n^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|\partial_{y}n^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)+C\|N\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}.

Proposition 3.2
.

There exist
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}-C\sigma\|\partial_{y}n\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\|c^{a}\|_{Y^{1}_{\infty}}+\|\nabla c^{a}\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1}}+\|n^{a}\|_{Y^{1}_{\infty}}+\|u^{a}\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1}}+\varepsilon^{2}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,3}}\right)\times

(3.25)

\displaystyle\quad\left(\|n\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C}{\sigma}\left(\|n\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}\right)^{3}+C\|K\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
m\geq 2\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exist
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+(1-C\delta-C\sigma)\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla^{2}c\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}-C\sigma\|\nabla n\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{\sigma}\biggl(1+\|u^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}+\|\nabla u^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}_{\infty}}+\|c^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}_{\infty}}+\|\nabla c^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}+\|\nabla n^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle+\|n^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}_{\infty}}+\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}\biggr)^{2}\left(\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|n\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\sigma^{3}\varepsilon^{4}}\left(\|c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|n\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle+C_{\delta}\varepsilon^{4}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}^{2}\left(1+\|\nabla c^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}c^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)+C\|\nabla K\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}.

Proposition 3.4
.

There exist
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}(1-C\sigma)\|\nabla u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sigma}\varepsilon^{4}\left(\|Fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{2,2}}^{2}+\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{2,3}}^{2}\right)+C\Bigg(\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{4}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,3}}^{2}\Bigg)^{\!3}

\displaystyle+C\left(1+\|u^{a}\|_{Y^{1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|\nabla u^{a}\|_{Y^{1}_{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left(\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|n\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{4}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+C\|U\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}.

For
\partial_{y}u
, if we directly compute the norm of
\nabla u
in the conormal Sobolev space, then higher-order terms will appear. Therefore, motivated by
[
24
,
40
]
, we use the vorticity equation to gain one normal derivative.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
m\geq 2\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exist
\delta>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\widehat{\omega}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+(1-C\delta-C\sigma)\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla\widehat{\omega}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}-C\sigma\|\nabla n\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}

(3.28)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{\sigma}\left(1+\|u^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|u^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|\omega^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}^{2}\right)\left(\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\widehat{\omega}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{4}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\sigma\varepsilon^{4}}\left(\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\widehat{\omega}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{4}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)^{3}+C\|\nabla^{\perp}\cdot U\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}

\displaystyle+C\varepsilon^{4}\left(\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+3}}^{2}+\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{2,m+2}}^{2}\right)\times\left(1+\|\omega^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|\partial_{y}\omega^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right),

where
\widehat{\omega}=\omega-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}fe^{-y}
.

3.2.
Proof of Theorem
2.2

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

We introduce the energy functional

\displaystyle E(t)=\|n\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|c\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla c\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\widehat{\omega}\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{3},

and

\displaystyle D(t)=\|\nabla n\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla^{2}c\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla\widehat{\omega}\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}.

Combining the estimates of Propositions
3.1
to
3.5
together, letting
m=2
and
\sigma,\delta
small enough, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E(t)+D(t)\leq C(t)E(t)+\frac{C^{\prime}}{\varepsilon^{4}}E(t)^{3},

(3.29)

where

\displaystyle C(t)=

\displaystyle C\biggl(1+\left\|n^{a}\right\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1,3}}+\|\nabla n^{a}\|_{Y^{1,2}_{\infty}}+\left\|u^{a}\right\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1,4}}+\left\|\nabla u^{a}\right\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1,3}}+\left\|\omega^{a}\right\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1,3}}+\left\|c^{a}\right\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1,4}}+\left\|\nabla c^{a}\right\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1,3}}\biggr)^{6}

\displaystyle+C\varepsilon\|fe^{-y}\|^{2}_{Y^{2,5}}\left(\left\|\partial_{y}^{2}c^{a}\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|\partial_{y}^{2}u^{a}\right\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+C\varepsilon\|Fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{2,2}}^{2}+C\big(1+\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{2,5}}\big)^{6}

\displaystyle+C\left(\|N\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|K\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla K\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|U\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{\perp}\cdot U\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}\right),

and we used

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}\leq 2\|\widehat{\omega}\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}+2\varepsilon^{4}\|\partial_{x}fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2},

(3.30)

\displaystyle\frac{C^{\prime}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\left(2\varepsilon^{4}\|\partial_{x}fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,2}}^{2}\right)^{3}\leq C\varepsilon^{8}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,3}}^{6},

and the definition of (
2.18
):

\displaystyle\left\|\cdot\right\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1}}\leq\left\|\cdot\right\|_{Y_{\infty}^{1,k}},\quad k\geq 1.

Then by (
3.29
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{d}{dt}E}{E^{3}}\leq\frac{C(t)}{E^{2}}+\frac{C^{\prime}}{\varepsilon^{4}},

and

\displaystyle E(t)^{-2}\geq\left(E(0)^{-2}-\frac{2C^{\prime}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\int_{0}^{\tau}C(\xi)d\xi}d\tau\right)e^{-2\int_{0}^{t}C(\tau)d\tau},

which implies

\displaystyle E(t)\leq\sqrt{\frac{e^{2\int_{0}^{t}C(\tau)d\tau}}{E(0)^{-2}-\frac{2C^{\prime}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\int_{0}^{\tau}C(\xi)d\xi}d\tau}}.

Noting that
E(0)=\varepsilon^{3}

and the integrability of
C(t)
in Proposition
2.1
, we deduce that

\displaystyle E\leq\varepsilon^{3}\frac{e^{\int_{0}^{t}C(\tau)d\tau}}{\sqrt{1-2C^{\prime}\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\int_{0}^{\tau}C(\xi)d\xi}d\tau}}.

Thus, there exist constants
C,T_{*}>0
, both independent of
\varepsilon
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T^{*}}E(t)\leq C\varepsilon^{3}\quad\text{for all }\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),

which implies

\displaystyle\|n\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|c\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla c\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\widehat{\omega}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{3},\quad\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),~t\in[0,T_{*}].

(3.31)

Using (
3.30
), Lemma
5.4
and Proposition
2.1
, we get

\displaystyle\|{\omega}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}^{2}\leq C,\quad\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}Y^{1,2}}^{2}\leq C.

(3.32)

Finally, it follows from (
3.32
), (
3.31
)
and Lemma
5.2
that

\displaystyle\|(n,c,u)\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T_{*};L_{xy}^{\infty})}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}.

The proof is complete.

MM

\square

4.
Construction of the approximate solution

In this section, we will construct the approximate solutions of the chemotaxis Navier-Stokes equations (
1.7
) by using the matched asymptotic expansion method(see also
[
37
,
8
]
).

4.1.
Asymptotic expansion

As in
[
37
,
8
]
), it is natural to choose the following ansatz.

\left\{\begin{aligned} &n^{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\left(n^{e,j}(t,x,y)+n^{b,j}(t,x,z)\right),\\
&c^{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\left(c^{e,j}(t,x,y)+c^{b,j}(t,x,z)\right),\\
&u^{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\left(u^{e,j}(t,x,y)+u^{b,j}(t,x,z)\right),\\
&p^{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\left(p^{e,j}(t,x,y)+p^{b,j}(t,x,z)\right),\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(4.33)

where
z=\frac{y}{\varepsilon}
and
n^{b,j},c^{b,j},u^{b,j},p^{b,j}
with their derivatives are vanishing exponentially when
z\to\infty
.

Substituting (
4.33
) into (
1.7
), we get

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(n^{e,j}+n^{b,j})+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(u^{e,j}+u^{b,j})\cdot\nabla\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(n^{e,j}+n^{b,j})

\displaystyle\quad-\Delta\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(n^{e,j}+n^{b,j})+\nabla\cdot\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(n^{e,j}+n^{b,j})\nabla\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(c^{e,j}+c^{b,j})\right)=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(c^{e,j}+c^{b,j})+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(u^{e,j}+u^{b,j})\cdot\nabla\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(c^{e,j}+c^{b,j})

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(c^{e,j}+c^{b,j})+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(c^{e,j}+c^{b,j})\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(n^{e,j}+n^{b,j})=0,

(4.34)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(u^{e,j}+u^{b,j})+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(u^{e,j}+u^{b,j})\cdot\nabla\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(u^{e,j}+u^{b,j})

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(p^{e,j}+p^{b,j})-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(u^{e,j}+u^{b,j})-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(n^{e,j}+n^{b,j})e_{2}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(u^{e,j}+u^{b,j})=0.

Away from the boundary, by taking
z\to\infty
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\partial_{t}n^{e,j}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}u^{e,j}\cdot\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\nabla n^{e,j}+\nabla\cdot\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}n^{e,j}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\nabla c^{e,j}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\Delta n^{e,j}=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\partial_{t}c^{e,j}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}u^{e,j}\cdot\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\nabla c^{e,j}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}c^{e,j}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}n^{e,j}

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j+2}\Delta c^{e,j}=0,

(4.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\partial_{t}u^{e,j}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}u^{e,j}\cdot\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\nabla u^{e,j}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\nabla p^{e,j}-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}n^{e,j}e_{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j+2}\Delta u^{e,j}=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\nabla\cdot u^{e,j}=0,

by noting that
(n^{b,j},c^{b,j},u^{b,j},p^{b,j})\to 0
when
z\to\infty
. Therefore,
(n^{e,j},c^{e,j},u^{e,j},p^{e,j})
with
j\geq 0
solves

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{e,j}+\sum_{k=0}^{j}u^{e,j-k}\cdot\nabla n^{e,k}-\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla n^{e,j}-\sum_{k=0}^{j}n^{e,j-k}\nabla c^{e,k}\right)=0,\\
&\partial_{t}c^{e,j}+\sum_{k=0}^{j}u^{e,j-k}\cdot\nabla c^{e,k}-\Delta c^{e,j-2}=-\sum_{k=0}^{j}c^{e,j-k}n^{e,k},\\
&\partial_{t}u^{e,j}+\sum_{k=0}^{j}u^{e,j-k}\cdot\nabla u^{e,k}+\nabla p^{e,j}-\Delta u^{e,j-2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
n^{e,j}\end{pmatrix},\\
&\nabla\cdot u^{e,j}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.36)

To analyze the behavior of the equations near the boundary, it is necessary to unify the coordinates into
z
by subtracting the outer layers (
4.1
) from the expanded equations (
4.1
). Assume that the boundary operator is expressed as
\overline{h}(t,x)=h(t,x,y)|_{y=0}
, and a Taylor expansion of
h(t,x,y)
near
y=0
is as follows:

\displaystyle h(t,x,y)=\sum_{l\geq 0}\varepsilon^{l}\frac{z^{l}}{l!}\overline{\partial_{y}^{l}h}(t,x).

(4.37)

Moreover, define

\left\{\begin{aligned} &n^{E,j}=\sum_{l=0}^{j}\frac{z^{l}}{l!}\overline{\partial_{y}^{l}n^{e,j-l}},\\
&c^{E,j}=\sum_{l=0}^{j}\frac{z^{l}}{l!}\overline{\partial_{y}^{l}c^{e,j-l}},\\
&u^{E,j}=\sum_{l=0}^{j}\frac{z^{l}}{l!}\overline{\partial_{y}^{l}u^{e,j-l}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.38)

Then we have the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

There holds

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\partial_{t}n^{b,j}+\sum_{j\geq-1}\varepsilon^{j}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\left((u^{E,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{E,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j\geq-2}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\left(\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,j}-\sum_{k=0}^{j}\left(n^{E,j-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}+n^{b,j-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,k}+n^{b,j-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}\right)\right)_{pp}=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\partial_{t}c^{b,j}+\sum_{j\geq-1}\varepsilon^{j}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\left((u^{E,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,j-2})_{pp}=-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\sum_{k=0}^{j}\left(c^{E,j-k}n^{b,k}+c^{b,j-k}n^{E,k}+c^{b,j-k}n^{b,k}\right),

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\partial_{t}u^{b,j}+\sum_{j\geq-1}\varepsilon^{j}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\left((u^{E,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{E,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j\geq-1}\varepsilon^{j}(\widehat{\nabla}p^{b,j})_{p}-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,j-2})_{pp}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}n^{b,j}e_{2},

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq-1}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(u^{b,j})_{p}=0,

where for a vector-valued function
(h_{1}(\cdot,j),h_{2}(\cdot,j))
we write

\binom{h_{1}(\cdot,j)}{h_{2}(\cdot,j)}_{p}=\binom{h_{1}(\cdot,j)}{h_{2}(\cdot,j+1)},~\binom{h_{1}(\cdot,j)}{h_{2}(\cdot,j)}_{pp}=\binom{h_{1}(\cdot,j)}{h_{2}(\cdot,j+2)},

(4.39)

and for a function let
h(\cdot,j)=0
when
j<0
(for example,
h(\cdot,j)=n^{b,j},c^{b,j},u_{1}^{e,j},u_{1}^{b,j}
etc).

Proof.

For a function
h^{e,j}(t,x,y)
, by (
4.38
) noting that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{e,j}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\sum_{l\geq 0}\varepsilon^{l}\frac{z^{l}}{l!}\overline{\partial_{y}^{l}h^{e,j}}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\sum_{l=0}^{j}\frac{z^{l}}{l!}\overline{\partial_{y}^{l}h^{e,j-l}}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{E,j},

and
then we have

(a)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(h^{e,j}+h^{b,j})\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(g^{e,j}+g^{b,j})-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{e,j}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}g^{e,j}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\sum_{k=0}^{j}\left(h^{E,j-k}g^{b,k}+h^{b,j-k}g^{E,k}+h^{b,j-k}g^{b,k}\right),

(b)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(h^{e,j}+h^{b,j})\partial_{y}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(g^{e,j}+g^{b,j})-\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{e,j}\partial_{y}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}g^{e,j}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{E,j}\partial_{z}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j-1}g^{b,j}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{b,j}\partial_{z}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j-1}g^{E,j}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{b,j}\partial_{z}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j-1}g^{b,j}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{j\geq-1}\varepsilon^{j}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\left(h^{E,{j+1}-k}\partial_{z}g^{b,k}+h^{b,{j+1}-k}\partial_{z}g^{E,k}+h^{b,{j+1}-k}\partial_{z}g^{b,k}\right),

(c)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(h^{e,j}+h^{b,j})\partial_{y}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(g^{e,j}+g^{b,j})\right)-\partial_{y}\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{e,j}\partial_{y}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}g^{e,j}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\partial_{y}\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{E,j}\partial_{y}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}g^{b,j}\right)+\partial_{y}\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{b,j}\partial_{y}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}g^{E,j}\right)+\partial_{y}\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{b,j}\partial_{y}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}g^{b,j}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{j\geq-2}\varepsilon^{j}\partial_{z}\sum_{k=0}^{j+2}\left(h^{E,{j+2}-k}\partial_{z}g^{b,k}+h^{b,{j+2}-k}\partial_{z}g^{E,k}+h^{b,{j+2}-k}\partial_{z}g^{b,k}\right),

and
(d)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}(h^{e,j}+h^{b,j})-\partial_{y}^{2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}h^{e,j}=\sum_{j\geq-2}\varepsilon^{j}\partial_{z}^{2}h^{b,j}.

Applying the above equalities, the proof of Lemma
4.1
is complete.

MM

\square

It follows from Lemma
4.1
immediately that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{b,j}+\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\left((u^{E,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{E,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}\right)\\
&\quad-\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\left(\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,j}-\sum_{k=0}^{j}\left(n^{E,j-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}+n^{b,j-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,k}+n^{b,j-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}\right)\right)_{pp}=0,\quad j\geq-2\\
&\partial_{t}c^{b,j}+\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\left((u^{E,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}\right)\\
&\quad-\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,j-2})_{pp}=-\sum_{k=0}^{j}\left(c^{E,j-k}n^{b,k}+c^{b,j-k}n^{E,k}+c^{b,j-k}n^{b,k}\right),\quad j\geq-1\\
&\partial_{t}u^{b,j}+\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\left((u^{E,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{E,k}+(u^{b,j-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}\right)\\
&\quad+(\widehat{\nabla}p^{b,j})_{p}-\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,j-2})_{pp}=n^{b,j}e_{2},\quad j\geq-1\\
&\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(u^{b,j})_{p}=0,\quad j\geq-1.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.40)

Moreover,
for the boundary conditions, plugging (
4.33
) into (
1.7
)
5
, we get

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{y}n^{e,j}+\partial_{z}n^{b,j+1}=0\quad\text{on}~y=z=0,\\
&\partial_{y}c^{e,j}+\partial_{z}c^{b,j+1}=0\quad\text{on}~y=z=0,\\
&\partial_{y}u_{1}^{e,j}+\partial_{z}u_{1}^{b,j+1}=0\quad\text{on}~y=z=0,\\
&u_{2}^{e,j}+u_{2}^{b,j}=0\quad\text{on}~y=z=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.41)

where
j\geq-1.

4.2.
Derivation of the matching terms

We will follow a step-by-step procedure.

Step I. The order
O(\varepsilon^{-2})
terms.
Taking
j=-2
in the system (
4.40
), and noting that
\partial_{z}c^{E,0}=0
due to (
4.38
) we have

\displaystyle-\partial_{z}\left(\partial_{z}n^{b,0}-n^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,0}-n^{b,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,0}\right)=0,

which implies that

\displaystyle\partial_{z}n^{b,0}-n^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,0}-n^{b,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,0}=0,

(4.42)

by noting that the vanishing exponential decay of the above boundary-layer functions at
\infty.

Using (
4.42
), it follow that
\partial_{z}(n^{E,0}+n^{b,0})=(n^{E,0}+n^{b,0})\partial_{z}c^{b,0}
. Then by solving first-order linear ordinary differential equations,
we have

n^{b,0}=n^{E,0}\left(e^{c^{b,0}}-1\right),

(4.43)

since
n^{b,0},c^{b,0}
decay fastly when
z\to\infty
.

Step II. The order
O(\varepsilon^{-1})
terms.
Taking
j=-1
in the system (
4.40
), and using (
4.38
) again we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} &u_{2}^{E,0}\partial_{z}n^{b,0}+u_{2}^{b,0}\partial_{z}n^{b,0}\\
&\quad-\partial_{z}\left(\partial_{z}n^{b,1}-\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left(n^{E,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}+n^{b,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{E,k}+n^{b,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}\right)\right)=0,\\
&u_{2}^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,0}+u_{2}^{b,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,0}=0,\\
&u_{2}^{E,0}\partial_{z}u^{b,0}+u_{2}^{b,0}\partial_{z}u^{b,0}+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\partial_{z}p^{b,0}\end{pmatrix}=0,\\
&\partial_{z}u_{2}^{b,0}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.44)

which implies

u_{2}^{b,0}\equiv 0,

(4.45)

since
\lim_{z\to\infty}u_{2}^{b,0}=0
. Using (
4.41
), we have

u_{2}^{E,0}=\overline{u_{2}^{e,0}}\equiv 0,

(4.46)

and it follows from (
4.44
)
3
that

p^{b,0}\equiv 0.

(4.47)

Using (
4.44
)
1
, (
4.45
) and (
4.46
) we have

\partial_{z}\left(\partial_{z}n^{b,1}-\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left(n^{E,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}+n^{b,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{E,k}+n^{b,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}\right)\right)=0,

which implies

\displaystyle\partial_{z}n^{b,1}-\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left(n^{E,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}+n^{b,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{E,k}+n^{b,1-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}\right)=0.

(4.48)

Using (
4.41
) with
j=-1
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}n^{b,0}=\partial_{z}c^{b,0}=\partial_{z}u_{1}^{b,0}=0\quad\text{on}~z=0.

(4.49)

Letting
z=0
in (
4.48
), by (
4.49
) we have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}n^{b,1}=n^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}+n^{b,0}(\overline{\partial_{y}c^{e,0}}+\partial_{z}c^{b,1})\quad\text{on}~z=0,

which and (

) with

yield that

\displaystyle\partial_{y}n^{e,0}-n^{e,0}\partial_{y}c^{e,0}=0\quad\text{on}~y=0.

(4.50)

Step III. The order
O(\varepsilon^{0})
terms.
It follows from (

) with

, (

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{e,0}+u^{e,0}\cdot\nabla n^{e,0}-\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla n^{e,0}-n^{e,0}\nabla c^{e,0}\right)=0,\\
&\partial_{t}c^{e,0}+u^{e,0}\cdot\nabla c^{e,0}+c^{e,0}n^{e,0}=0,\\
&\partial_{t}u^{e,0}+u^{e,0}\cdot\nabla u^{e,0}+\nabla p^{e,0}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
n^{e,0}\end{pmatrix},\\
&\nabla\cdot u^{e,0}=0,\\
&\partial_{y}n^{e,0}-n^{e,0}\partial_{y}c^{e,0}=0,\quad u_{2}^{e,0}=0\quad\text{on}~y=0\\
&(n^{e,0},c^{e,0},u^{e,0})(0,x,y)=(n_{in},c_{in},u_{in}).\end{aligned}\right.

(4.51)

By the uniqueness of the solution, we conclude that this coincides with the equation (
1.8
) when
\varepsilon=0
.

For the boundary layer analysis,
taking
j=0
in the system (
4.40
) we get

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{b,0}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left((u^{E,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}+(u^{b,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{E,k}+(u^{b,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}\right)\\
&\quad-\partial_{x}\left(\partial_{x}n^{b,0}-\left(n^{E,0}\partial_{x}c^{b,0}+n^{b,0}\partial_{x}c^{E,0}+n^{b,0}\partial_{x}c^{b,0}\right)\right)\\
&\quad-\partial_{z}\left(\partial_{z}n^{b,2}-\sum_{k=0}^{2}\left(n^{E,2-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}+n^{b,2-k}\partial_{z}c^{E,k}+n^{b,2-k}\partial_{z}c^{b,k}\right)\right)=0,\\
&\partial_{t}c^{b,0}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left((u^{E,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}+(u^{b,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,k}+(u^{b,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}\right)-\partial_{z}^{2}c^{b,0}\\
&\quad=-\left(c^{E,0}n^{b,0}+c^{b,0}n^{E,0}+c^{b,0}n^{b,0}\right),\\
&\partial_{t}u^{b,0}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left((u^{E,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}+(u^{b,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{E,k}+(u^{b,-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}\right)\\
&\quad+(\widehat{\nabla}p^{b,0})_{p}-\partial_{z}^{2}u^{b,0}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
n^{b,0}\end{pmatrix},\\
&\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(u^{b,0})_{p}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|c^{b,0}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{z}c^{b,0}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq-\int_{R}n^{E,0}c^{E,0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(e^{c^{b,0}}-1\right)c^{b,0}\,dydx-\int_{R}n^{E,0}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{c^{b,0}}\left(c^{b,0}\right)^{2}\,dydx,

due to

and

which implies

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{b,1}+\sum_{k=0}^{2}\left((u^{E,1-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}+(u^{b,1-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{E,k}+(u^{b,1-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}\right)\\

&\quad=-\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left(c^{E,1-k}n^{b,k}+c^{b,1-k}n^{E,k}+c^{b,1-k}n^{b,k}\right),\\
&\partial_{t}u^{b,1}+\sum_{k=0}^{2}\left((u^{E,1-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}+(u^{b,1-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{E,k}+(u^{b,1-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}\right)\\
&\quad+(\widehat{\nabla}p^{b,1})_{p}-\partial_{z}^{2}u^{b,1}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
n^{b,1}\end{pmatrix},\\
&\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(u^{b,1})_{p}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.64)

Combining (
4.64
), the boundary condition (

), (

), (

) and (

), we deduce that
u_{1}^{b,1},u_{2}^{b,2}

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u_{1}^{b,1}+\left(u^{E,0}\right)_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u_{1}^{b,1}+u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}u_{1}^{E,0}-\partial_{z}^{2}u_{1}^{b,1}=0,\\
&\partial_{x}u_{1}^{b,1}+\partial_{z}u_{2}^{b,2}=0,\\
&\partial_{z}u_{1}^{b,1}=-\overline{\partial_{y}u_{1}^{e,0}}\quad\text{on}~z=0,\\
&\lim_{z\to\infty}u_{1}^{b,1}=\lim_{z\to\infty}u_{2}^{b,2}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.65)

Similarly, by (
4.48
) we have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}n^{b,1}-n^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}=0,

which implies

\displaystyle n^{b,1}-n^{E,0}c^{b,1}=0

by applying the integration with respect to
z
. Using this,
\eqref{eq:b1}_{2}

and (
4.41
) we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}c^{b,1}+u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}c^{E,0}+(u^{E,0})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,1}-\partial_{z}^{2}c^{b,1}=-\left(c^{b,1}n^{E,0}+c^{E,0}n^{b,1}\right),\\
&n^{b,1}-n^{E,0}c^{b,1}=0,\\
&\partial_{z}c^{b,1}=-\overline{\partial_{y}c^{e,0}}\quad\text{on}~z=0,\\
&\lim_{z\to\infty}c^{b,1}=\lim_{z\to\infty}n^{b,1}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.66)

Moreover, by (
4.64
)
p^{b,2}
satisfies

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{z}p^{b,2}=n^{b,1},\\
&\lim_{z\to\infty}p^{b,2}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

which implies

p^{b,2}=-\int_{z}^{\infty}n^{b,1}(t,x,\zeta)d\zeta.

(4.67)

Step V. The order
O(\varepsilon^{2})
terms.

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{b,2}+\sum_{k=0}^{3}\left((u^{E,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}+(u^{b,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{E,k}+(u^{b,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,k}\right)\\
&-\widehat{\nabla}\cdot\left(\widehat{\nabla}n^{b,2}-\sum_{k=0}^{2}\left(n^{E,2-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}+n^{b,2-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,k}+n^{b,2-k}\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}\right)\right)_{pp}=0,\\
&\partial_{t}c^{b,2}+\sum_{k=0}^{3}\left((u^{E,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}+(u^{b,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,k}+(u^{b,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,k}\right)\\
&-\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,0})_{pp}=-\sum_{k=0}^{2}\left(c^{E,2-k}n^{b,k}+c^{b,2-k}n^{E,k}+c^{b,2-k}n^{b,k}\right),\\
&\partial_{t}u^{b,2}+\sum_{k=0}^{3}\left((u^{E,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}+(u^{b,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{E,k}+(u^{b,2-k})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,k}\right)\\
&+(\widehat{\nabla}p^{b,2})_{p}-\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(\widehat{\nabla}u^{b,0})_{pp}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
n^{b,2}\end{pmatrix},\\
&\widehat{\nabla}\cdot(u^{b,2})_{p}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.68)

thus

u_{1}^{b,2}
satisfy

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u_{1}^{b,2}+\Big((u^{E,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u_{1}^{b,1}+(u^{b,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u_{1}^{E,1}+(u^{b,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u_{1}^{b,1}\Big)\\
&+(u^{E,0})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}u_{1}^{b,2}+u_{1}^{b,2}\partial_{x}u_{1}^{E,0}+\partial_{x}p^{b,2}-\partial_{z}^{2}u_{1}^{b,2}=0,\\
&\partial_{z}u_{1}^{b,2}=0,\quad z=0,\\
&\lim_{z\to\infty}u_{1}^{b,2}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.69)

where we used (
4.63
), (
4.60
) and (
4.41
).

Moreover, by (
4.57
),
\eqref{eq:b2}_{2}
and (
4.41
)

n^{b,2},c^{b,2}
satisfy

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}c^{b,2}+(u^{E,0})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,2}+u_{1}^{b,2}\partial_{x}c^{E,0}-\partial_{z}^{2}c^{b,2}\\
&+\left((u^{E,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,1}+(u^{b,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,1}+(u^{b,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,1}\right)\\
&+c^{b,2}n^{E,0}+\left(c^{E,1}n^{b,1}+c^{b,1}n^{E,1}+c^{b,1}n^{b,1}\right)+c^{E,0}n^{b,2}=0,\\
&\partial_{z}n^{b,2}=\left(n^{E,1}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}+n^{b,1}\partial_{z}c^{E,1}+n^{b,1}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}\right)+n^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,2},\\
&\partial_{z}c^{b,2}=0\quad\text{on}~z=0,\\
&\lim_{z\to\infty}c^{b,2}=\lim_{z\to\infty}n^{b,2}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.70)

4.3.
Combination of the solutions

Based on the definition of the approximate solution (

), we have the following lemma:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let

f(t,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{x}u_{1}^{b,1}(t,x,z)\,dz=u_{2}^{b,2}(t,x,0),

(4.71)

then
(n^{a},c^{a},u^{a},p^{a})

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}n^{a}+u^{a}\cdot\nabla n^{a}-\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla n^{a}-n^{a}\nabla c^{a}\right)=-N,\\

&\partial_{t}u^{a}+u^{a}\cdot\nabla u^{a}+\nabla p^{a}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta u^{a}-\begin{pmatrix}0\\
n^{a}\end{pmatrix}=-U,\\
&\nabla\cdot u^{a}=0,\\
&\partial_{y}n^{a}=\partial_{y}c^{a}=\partial_{y}u_{1}^{a}=0,\quad u_{2}^{a}=\varepsilon^{2}f(t,x)\quad\text{on}~y=0,\end{aligned}\right.

where

is given by:

\displaystyle-N=

\displaystyle\varepsilon\partial_{t}n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}n^{b,2}+\left(u_{1}^{e,0}\partial_{x}(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2})+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}n^{e,0}+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2})\right)

(4.73)

\displaystyle+\left((u_{2}^{e,0}-u_{2}^{E,0})\partial_{z}(n^{b,1}+\varepsilon n^{b,2})+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{y}n^{e,0}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{z}(n^{b,1}+\varepsilon n^{b,2})\right)

\displaystyle-\partial_{x}\left(\partial_{x}(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2})-n^{e,0}\partial_{x}(\varepsilon c^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2})-(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2})\partial_{x}c^{e,0}\right)

\displaystyle-(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2})\partial_{x}(\varepsilon c^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2})

\displaystyle-\partial_{y}\left(\left(y\overline{\partial_{y}n^{e,0}}+n^{E,0}-n^{e,0}\right)\partial_{z}c^{b,1}+\varepsilon n^{b,1}\left(\overline{\partial_{y}c^{e,0}}-\partial_{y}c^{e,0}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-\partial_{y}\left(\varepsilon(n^{E,0}-n^{e,0})\partial_{z}c^{b,2}-\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\left(\partial_{y}c^{e,0}+\partial_{z}c^{b,1}\right)-\varepsilon(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2})\partial_{z}c^{b,2}\right),

and

\displaystyle-K=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(u_{1}^{e,0}-\overline{u_{1}^{e,0}})\partial_{x}c^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}(u_{1}^{e,0}-\overline{u_{1}^{e,0}})\partial_{x}c^{b,2}+(u_{2}^{e,0}-y\overline{\partial_{y}u_{2}^{e,0}})\partial_{z}c^{b,1}

(4.74)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon(u_{2}^{e,0}-y\overline{\partial_{y}u_{2}^{e,0}})\partial_{z}c^{b,2}-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{y}^{2}c^{e,0}+\varepsilon(c^{e,0}-\overline{c^{e,0}})n^{b,1}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}(c^{e,0}-c^{E,0})n^{b,2}+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}(c^{e,0}-\overline{c^{e,0}})+\varepsilon^{3}u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}c^{b,2}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}(\partial_{y}c^{e,0}-\overline{\partial_{y}c^{e,0}})+\varepsilon^{3}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{z}c^{b,2}-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}c^{a}+\varepsilon^{3}c^{b,1}n^{b,2}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2})+\varepsilon c^{b,1}(n^{e,0}-n^{E,0})+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}(n^{e,0}-n^{E,0})

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{2}\left((u^{E,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,1}+u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}c^{E,1}+u_{1}^{b,2}\partial_{x}c^{E,0}+c^{E,1}n^{b,1}+c^{b,1}n^{E,1}\right),

and

\displaystyle-U_{1}=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(u_{1}^{e,0}-\overline{u_{1}^{e,0}})\partial_{x}u_{1}^{b,1}+(u_{2}^{e,0}-y\overline{\partial_{y}u_{2}^{e,0}})\partial_{z}u_{1}^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}p^{b,2}-\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{x}^{2}u_{1}^{b,1}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta u_{1}^{e,0}

(4.75)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}(u_{1}^{e,0}-\overline{u_{1}^{e,0}})+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{y}u_{1}^{a}+\varepsilon^{2}u^{b,1}_{1}\partial_{x}u^{b,1}_{1},

and

\displaystyle-U_{2}=

(4.76)

\displaystyle+\left(\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{y}u_{2}^{e,0}+\varepsilon(u_{2}^{e,0}-u_{2}^{E,0})\partial_{z}u_{2}^{b,2}+\varepsilon^{3}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{z}u_{2}^{b,2}\right)-\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}.

Proof.

For the term
N
, by (
4.51
) we get

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(n^{e,0}+\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\{\left(u_{1}^{e,0}+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}\right)\partial_{x}\left(n^{e,0}+\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)+\left(u_{2}^{e,0}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}\right)\partial_{y}\left(n^{e,0}+\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla\cdot\biggl\{\nabla\left(n^{e,0}+\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)-\left(n^{e,0}+\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\nabla\left(c^{e,0}+\varepsilon c^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}\right)\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\partial_{t}n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{t}n^{b,2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(u_{1}^{e,0}\partial_{x}\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}n^{e,0}+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(u_{2}^{e,0}\partial_{y}\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{y}n^{e,0}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{y}\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla\cdot\biggl\{\nabla\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)-n^{e,0}\nabla\left(\varepsilon c^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}\right)-\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\nabla c^{e,0}

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\nabla\left(\varepsilon c^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}\right)\biggr\}.

Furthermore, by
\eqref{eq:c^b1,n^b1}_{2}
and
\eqref{eq:c^b2,n^b2}_{2}
we have

\displaystyle-\partial_{y}\biggl\{\partial_{y}\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)-n^{e,0}\partial_{y}\left(\varepsilon c^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}\right)-\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\partial_{y}c^{e,0}

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\partial_{y}\left(\varepsilon c^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}\right)\biggr\}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{y}\biggl\{\left(n^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}+\varepsilon\left(n^{E,1}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}+n^{b,1}\partial_{z}c^{E,1}+n^{b,1}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}+n^{E,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-n^{e,0}\partial_{z}\left(c^{b,1}+\varepsilon c^{b,2}\right)-\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\partial_{y}c^{e,0}-\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\partial_{z}\left(c^{b,1}+\varepsilon c^{b,2}\right)\biggr\}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{y}\biggl\{\left(y\overline{\partial_{y}n^{e,0}}+n^{E,0}-n^{e,0}\right)\partial_{z}c^{b,1}+\varepsilon n^{b,1}\left(\overline{\partial_{y}c^{e,0}}-\partial_{y}c^{e,0}\right)+\varepsilon\left(n^{E,0}-n^{e,0}\right)\partial_{z}c^{b,2}

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\left(\partial_{y}c^{e,0}+\partial_{z}c^{b,1}\right)-\varepsilon\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)\partial_{z}c^{b,2}\biggr\},

where we used (
4.63
), (
4.62
) and (

) in the last equality. Then the proof of (

) is complete due to (

).

For the term
K
, through
\eqref{eq:e0}_{2}
,
\eqref{eq:c^b1,n^b1}_{1}
and
\eqref{eq:c^b2,n^b2}_{1}
, it shows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c^{e,0}+u^{e,0}\cdot\nabla c^{e,0}+c^{e,0}n^{e,0}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\Bigl\{\partial_{t}c^{b,1}+\left(u_{1}^{E,0}\partial_{x}c^{b,1}+z\overline{\partial_{y}u_{2}^{e,0}}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}\right)+u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}c^{E,0}-\partial_{z}^{2}c^{b,1}

\displaystyle+\left(c^{E,0}n^{b,1}+c^{b,1}n^{E,0}\right)\Bigr\}+\varepsilon^{2}\Bigl\{\partial_{t}c^{b,2}+(u^{E,0})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,2}

\displaystyle+\left((u^{E,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,1}+(u^{b,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{E,1}+(u^{b,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,1}\right)+u_{1}^{b,2}\partial_{x}c^{E,0}-\partial_{z}^{2}c^{b,2}

\displaystyle+\left(c^{b,2}n^{E,0}+(c^{E,1}n^{b,1}+c^{b,1}n^{E,1}+c^{b,1}n^{b,1})+c^{E,0}n^{b,2}\right)\Bigr\}=0.

Then, by adding the required terms such as
\varepsilon u_{1}^{e,0}\partial_{x}c^{b,1},u_{2}^{e,0}\partial_{z}c^{b,1}
, combining the corresponding terms defined in (
2.20
) and moving the remainder to the right-hand side of the equation, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c^{a}+u_{1}^{a}\partial_{x}c^{a}+u_{2}^{a}\partial_{y}c^{a}+c^{a}n^{a}-\varepsilon^{2}\Delta c^{a}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\left(u_{2}^{e,0}-\varepsilon z\overline{\partial_{y}u_{2}^{e,0}}\right)\partial_{z}c^{b,2}-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{y}^{2}c^{e,0}+\varepsilon\left(c^{e,0}-c^{E,0}\right)n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}\left(c^{e,0}-c^{E,0}\right)n^{b,2}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}\left(c^{e,0}-c^{E,0}\right)+\varepsilon^{3}u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}c^{b,2}+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}^{b,2}\left(\partial_{y}c^{e,0}-\overline{\partial_{y}c^{e,0}}\right)+\varepsilon^{3}u_{2}^{b,2}\partial_{z}c^{b,2}

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}c^{a}+\varepsilon^{3}c^{b,1}n^{b,2}+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}\left(\varepsilon n^{b,1}+\varepsilon^{2}n^{b,2}\right)+\varepsilon c^{b,1}\left(n^{e,0}-n^{E,0}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}c^{b,2}\left(n^{e,0}-n^{E,0}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon^{2}\left\{\left((u^{E,1})_{p}\cdot\widehat{\nabla}c^{b,1}+u_{1}^{b,1}\partial_{x}c^{E,1}\right)+u_{1}^{b,2}\partial_{x}c^{E,0}+\left(c^{E,1}n^{b,1}+c^{b,1}n^{E,1}\right)\right\},

which along with (

) complete the proof of (
4.74
).

U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are similar by using (

), (

) and (

), and we omitted it.

\square

5.
Energy estimates

5.1.
Some important lemmas

The following lemmas are frequently used in our proof.

Lemma 5.1

(
[
15
]
)
.

Let
\{x_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{3}}
and
\{y_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{3}}
be real numbers. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=m}\sum_{|\beta|=j,\beta\leq\alpha}x_{\beta}y_{\alpha-\beta}=\left(\sum_{|\alpha|=j}x_{\alpha}\right)\left(\sum_{|\beta|=m-j}y_{\beta}\right).

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\left\|g\right\|_{Y^{1,m}}\leq C
, then the following inequality holds:

\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m-1}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}g\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq C\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}g\right\|_{2}\,\left\|\partial_{y}\partial^{\alpha}g\right\|_{2}.

(5.77)

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\left\|g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left\|\left\|g\right\|_{L_{x}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\partial_{x}g\right\|_{L_{x}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{L_{y}^{\infty}}\leq C\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\|\partial_{x}g\right\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\|\partial_{y}g\right\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\|\partial_{xy}g\right\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{4}}

and the proof of (
5.77
) is complete.

MM

\square

Lemma 5.3
.

Assume that
\overline{g}=0
and
\psi
is defined as in (
2.16
), then for
1<q\leq\infty
,

\left\|\frac{\partial^{\alpha}g}{\psi}\right\|_{q}\leq C_{\delta}\left(\left\|\partial_{y}\partial^{\alpha}g\right\|_{q}+\left\|\partial^{\alpha}g\right\|_{q}\right).

(5.78)

Proof.

Denote

\chi(y)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&0\leq y\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
&0,&y\geq 1.\end{aligned}\right.

Then by Hardy inequality (see, for example,
[
38
]
) with
1<q<\infty
and the Lipschitz continuity of
W^{1,\infty}
when
q=\infty
,

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\partial^{\alpha}g}{\psi}\right\|_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\chi(y)\frac{y}{\psi}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\frac{\partial^{\alpha}g}{y}\right\|_{q}+\left\|\frac{1-\chi(y)}{\psi}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}g\right\|_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\delta}\left(\left\|\partial_{y}\partial^{\alpha}g\right\|_{q}+\left\|\partial^{\alpha}g\right\|_{q}\right)

MM

\square

For the conormal space
Y^{l,m}
, we present the following Korn’s type inequality.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\nabla\cdot G=0
and

\omega^{g}=\partial_{y}g_{1}-\partial_{x}g_{2}
, where

G=(g_{1},g_{2})
and

\widehat{g_{2}}=g_{2}-\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y}
. For
1\leq m<\infty
, if
\delta
is small enough, then there holds

\|\nabla G\|_{Y^{1,m}}\leq C_{\delta}\left(\|\omega^{g}\|_{Y^{1,m}}+\|\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}\right).

(5.79)

Proof.

Due to
\nabla\cdot G=0
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta g_{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}^{2}g_{2}+\partial_{y}^{2}g_{2}=\partial_{x}^{2}g_{2}-\partial_{y}\partial_{x}g_{1}=-\partial_{x}\omega^{g},

and it follows that

\displaystyle-\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle\partial^{\alpha}\Delta g_{2},\partial^{\alpha}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}\omega^{g},\partial^{\alpha}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle.

Applying the absolute value inequality to the right-hand side yields

\displaystyle-\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle\partial^{\alpha}\Delta g_{2},\partial^{\alpha}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle\leq\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}|\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}\omega^{g},\partial^{\alpha}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle|.

(5.80)

By integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle\partial^{\alpha}\Delta g_{2},\partial^{\alpha}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}g_{2},\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle+\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{2},\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle-\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle[\partial^{\alpha},\partial_{y}]\partial_{y}g_{2},\partial^{\alpha}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle+\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{2},[\partial_{y},\partial^{\alpha}]\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle,

which is greater than

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}|\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}(\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y}),\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}g_{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}-\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}|\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{2},\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}(\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y})\rangle|

\displaystyle-C\delta\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}|\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{2},\partial^{\alpha-(0,0,1)}\partial_{y}g_{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle-C\delta\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}|\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{2},\partial^{\alpha-(0,0,1)}\partial_{y}(\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y})\rangle|.

Moreover, by Hölder’s inequality, it shows

\displaystyle-\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\langle\partial^{\alpha}\Delta g_{2},\partial^{\alpha}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq(1-C\sigma-C\delta)\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\nabla g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}-C\left(\delta+\frac{1}{\sigma}\right)\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\nabla(\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-C\delta\left(\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha-(0,0,1)}\partial_{y}g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq(1-C\sigma-C\delta)\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\nabla g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}-C\left(\delta+\frac{1}{\sigma}\right)\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\nabla(\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y})\|_{2}^{2}.

For the term on the right hand of (
5.80
), it shows

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}|\langle\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}\omega^{g},\partial^{\alpha}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle|=\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}|\langle\partial^{\alpha}\omega^{g},\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sigma}\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\omega^{g}\|_{2}^{2}+C\sigma\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+C\sigma\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{x}\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y})\|_{2}^{2}.

Summing up these estimates, we have

\displaystyle(1-C\sigma-C\delta)\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\nabla g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sigma}\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\omega^{g}\|_{2}^{2}+C\left(\delta+\frac{1}{\sigma}\right)\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m+1}\|\partial^{\alpha}(\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y})\|_{2}^{2}.

Letting
\sigma,\delta
small enough, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\nabla g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\omega^{g}\|_{2}^{2}+C\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m+1}\|\partial^{\alpha}(\overline{g_{2}}e^{-y})\|_{2}^{2}.

(5.81)

For
g_{1}
, as
\nabla\cdot G=0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\nabla g_{1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{x}g_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{y}g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}(\partial_{y}g_{1}-\partial_{x}g_{2}+\partial_{x}g_{2})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\nabla g_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\|\partial^{\alpha}\omega^{g}\|_{2}^{2}.

(5.82)

Combining (
5.81
) and (
5.1
), this proof is complete.

MM

\square

5.2.
Nonlinear estimates in conormal Sobolev spaces.

In this section, we deal with the product terms or nonlinear terms. For simplicity,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
means the inner
product in
L^{2}_{xy}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
.

First, for
H\cdot\nabla G
we have the following estimates.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
u^{a}
and
u
be as in (
2.10
) and (
2.11
). Then there holds

(a)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl\langle\partial^{\alpha}(u^{a}\cdot\nabla G),\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|

(5.83)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{\sigma}\left(1+\|u^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}+\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m}_{\infty}}\right)^{2}\|G\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+C\sigma\varepsilon^{4}\|\partial_{y}G\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2},

(b)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl\langle\partial^{\alpha}(u\cdot\nabla G^{a}),\partial^{\alpha}Q\bigr\rangle\bigr|

(5.84)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left(\|G^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+1\right)\|Q\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+C\varepsilon^{4}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}^{2}\left(\|G^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}_{\infty}}^{2}+\|\partial_{y}G^{a}\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right),

and

(c)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\bigl|\bigl\langle\partial^{\alpha}(u\cdot\nabla G),\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|

(5.85)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sigma}\left(\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|G\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)+\frac{C}{\sigma\varepsilon^{4}}\left(\|u\|_{Y^{1}}^{2}+\|\omega\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+\|G\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\sigma}\varepsilon^{2}\|fe^{-y}\|_{Y^{1,m+1}}^{2}\|G\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}+C\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla G\|_{Y^{1,m}}^{2}.

Proof.

(a) The term
\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\bigl|\bigl\langle\partial^{\alpha}(u^{a}\cdot\nabla G),\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|
is divided into two terms:

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\bigl|\bigl\langle\partial^{\alpha}(u^{a}\cdot\nabla G),\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\bigl|\bigl\langle u^{a}\cdot\partial^{\alpha}\nabla G,\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|+C\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha,|\beta|\leq m-1}\bigl|\bigl\langle\partial^{\alpha-\beta}u^{a}\cdot\partial^{\beta}\nabla G,\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|

\displaystyle=:a_{1}+a_{2}.

For
a_{1}
, by integration by parts and (
2.17
),

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\bigl|\bigl\langle u^{a}\cdot\nabla\partial^{\alpha}G,\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|+\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\bigl|\bigl\langle u_{2}^{a}[\partial^{\alpha},\partial_{y}]G,\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\Bigl|\int_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}f(\overline{\partial^{\alpha}G})^{2}\,dx\Bigr|+C\delta\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq 1,|\alpha|\leq m}\bigl|\bigl\langle u_{2}^{a}\partial^{\alpha-(0,0,1)}\partial_{y}G,\partial^{\alpha}G\bigr\rangle\bigr|

and by Hölder’s inequality and Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality, this becomes
